%==========================================================================
% CHAPTER 1 -- GENERAL INTRODUCTION
%==========================================================================
\chapter{General Introduction}\label{ch:intro}

\section{Background and Motivation}\label{sec:intro-background}

Hypertension is the leading modifiable risk factor for premature death worldwide. The first global report of the World Health Organization (WHO) devoted to the condition establishes that the number of adults aged 30--79 years living with hypertension doubled between 1990 and 2019, reaching 1.3 billion, that about 78\% of them live in low- and middle-income countries, and that elevated systolic blood pressure is responsible for an estimated 10.8 million deaths each year \parencite{who2023hypertension}. Sub-Saharan Africa combines a high and increasing prevalence with persistently poor detection, treatment and control \parencite{gafane2024}: in the WHO African Region, only 12\% of hypertensive adults have their blood pressure controlled.

Diabetes compounds this burden. In Africa, more than one person with diabetes in two is also hypertensive, and the highest sub-regional estimate concerns Central Africa, the region in which the present work is deployed \parencite{hinneh2023}. The two conditions share their risk factors, aggravate each other, and call for the same kind of care: repeated measurement and treatment adjustment sustained over decades.

At the same time, the technological environment of the region is changing. Mobile telephony is now widespread and health technology is among the leading digital investment sectors in sub-Saharan Africa \parencite{segun2025}, and a controlled study in Cameroonian district hospitals has shown that remote clinician support is feasible in primary care \parencite{bediang2021}. A care model that fails at follow-up, and an environment in which remote follow-up has become conceivable: these two observations motivate the present work.

\section{Problem Statement}\label{sec:intro-problem}\label{sec:scope-problem}

Care for hypertension and diabetes in Cameroon is episodic. Blood pressure and blood glucose are measured at consultations that occur a few times a year, recorded on paper or in fragmented registers, and rarely assembled into a history that a clinician can read as a trend. Yet diagnosis and treatment adjustment are longitudinal acts: the WHO protocol requires measurements repeated over several visits and monthly follow-up until the blood pressure target is reached \parencite{who2021guideline,who2023hypertension}. The consequences are loss to follow-up, which often affects half of patients or more in resource-limited settings, and therapeutic inertia, the failure to intensify treatment when blood pressure remains uncontrolled.

Telemonitoring could close this gap, but three obstacles stand in the way. First, the deployment environment imposes technological, organisational, financial, individual, cultural and regulatory barriers \parencite{dodoo2022}, and the comparative evidence on telemedicine adoption in the region comes from South Africa, Kenya and Nigeria rather than from Central Africa \parencite{agbeyangi2025}. Second, the clinical evidence that telemonitoring improves blood pressure and glycaemic control comes almost entirely from high-income settings. Third, a system that processes real patient data through a mobile and cloud pipeline must earn the trust of patients in the confidentiality and security of their data \parencite{alhammad2024}.

The research question addressed in this thesis is therefore the following: \emph{how can consultation data, home measurements and mobile sensor data be integrated into a telemonitoring system that detects the deterioration of patients with hypertension and diabetes early enough to support a clinical decision, under the infrastructural, organisational and regulatory constraints of the Cameroonian context?}

\section{Objectives}\label{sec:intro-objectives}

The general objective is to design a telemonitoring system for patients with hypertension and type~2 diabetes that combines consultation data, home measurements and, where available, mobile sensor data, and that supports the clinician through risk assessment and alert prioritisation. The specific objectives are:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item to synthesise the evidence on the burden of the two conditions, the effectiveness of telemonitoring, the enabling technologies, the predictive methods and the deployment constraints (\cref{ch:background}), and to derive from it the requirements of the system (\cref{ch:techsota});
  \item to construct a multi-source dataset with a documented schema, preprocessing pipeline and labelling rule, and to develop and evaluate predictive risk models on it (\cref{ch:data});
  \item to design the architecture of the system: acquisition, cloud backend, patient application and clinician dashboard (\cref{ch:architecture}).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Research Hypotheses}\label{sec:intro-hypotheses}

Three hypotheses, set out in the research framing document of this work, guide the experimental phase. They are confronted with the literature in \cref{sec:forecasting}.
\begin{description}[nosep]
  \item[H1.] A deep temporal model obtains better recall on high and critical risk states than robust classical models, on locally collected telemonitoring data.
  \item[H2.] Short-horizon forecasting of physiological parameters allows degradation events to be anticipated with a clinically useful lead time.
  \item[H3.] Given the limited size and irregularity of the local data, the gain of the deep model over classical models may remain modest.
\end{description}

\section{Organisation of the Thesis}\label{sec:intro-organisation}

\Cref{ch:background} reviews the literature in six themes, from the burden of disease to data protection, and states the research gap. \Cref{ch:techsota} derives from this review the clinical, acquisition, architectural, modelling and decision-support requirements of the system. \Cref{ch:data} describes data collection, preprocessing, feature engineering and predictive modelling. \Cref{ch:architecture} presents the architecture and software design of the system. The references cited throughout are listed in the bibliography at the end of the document.

%==========================================================================
% CHAPTER 2 -- LITERATURE REVIEW
%==========================================================================
\chapter{Literature Review}\label{ch:litreview}\label{ch:background}

\section*{Scope and Organisation of the Review}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Scope and Organisation of the Review}

This chapter reviews the literature on which the design of the proposed system rests. It draws on nineteen peer-reviewed sources, each checked against PubMed, PubMed Central, Crossref or the publisher's record, complemented by three institutional documents of the WHO \parencite{who2023hypertension,who2021guideline,whoHEARTS} and by the TELEMED-CAM study \parencite{kingue2013}, the earliest Cameroonian evaluation of hypertension telemanagement. The 2023 guidelines of the European Society of Hypertension (ESH) \parencite{mancia2023}, from which the blood pressure thresholds of the risk levels are taken, are cited wherever those thresholds are applied.

The sources are organised in six themes that follow the logic of the argument, from the problem to the response (\cref{tab:lr-overview}): the global burden of hypertension and diabetes (\cref{sec:burden}); hypertension care and digital health in sub-Saharan Africa (\cref{sec:lr-regional}); the clinical evidence for mobile health and telemonitoring (\cref{sec:outpatient}); sensor and Internet of Things (IoT) technologies (\cref{sec:lr-iot}); machine learning for risk prediction (\cref{sec:lr-ml}); and data privacy and ethics (\cref{sec:lr-privacy}). Within each theme, sub-themes go from the problem to the response, and the studies of each sub-theme are presented in chronological order of publication, so that the progression of the evidence is visible. Each study is presented once, in the theme to which it contributes most, and is only recalled elsewhere. \Cref{sec:positioning} synthesises the findings and states the research gap.

%##########################################################################
\section{Global Burden of Hypertension and Diabetes}\label{sec:burden}\label{sec:transition}\label{sec:hta-burden}

\subsection{The global burden of hypertension}

The WHO global report on hypertension \parencite{who2023hypertension} is the reference framing of the problem. For population surveillance it defines hypertension as a systolic blood pressure (SBP) $\geq 140$~mmHg, a diastolic blood pressure (DBP) $\geq 90$~mmHg, or the use of antihypertensive medication, in adults aged 30--79 years. This threshold is used throughout this thesis for epidemiological figures; the lower 130/80~mmHg classification of the ESH \parencite{mancia2023}, used later to define the risk levels, explains part of the discrepancies between publications.

The report establishes that the number of hypertensive adults rose from 650 million in 1990 to 1.3 billion in 2019, while the age-standardised prevalence barely changed, from 32\% to 33\%: the increase in absolute numbers is mostly explained by population growth and ageing rather than by a rise in individual risk. Elevated systolic blood pressure is the leading risk factor for premature death among the eighty-seven factors compared, ahead of smoking and hyperglycaemia, with 10.8 million deaths and 235 million disability-adjusted life years each year. Progress towards the global target is insufficient: the prevalence of uncontrolled hypertension fell only from 29\% in 2010 to 26\% in 2019, and no WHO region is on track. The report notes, however, that the target would be met if about half of the adults already diagnosed were effectively treated. Among the recommended responses, it endorses team-based care, community and home blood pressure measurement, and telemonitoring.

As an institutional synthesis, the report relies on modelled estimates and on references that it does not detail; it does not address patient-level monitoring technology. It is used here for the global framing and for its normative positions, not as primary evidence.

\subsection{The failure of the care cascade}\label{sec:cascade}

The WHO structures the care of hypertension as a cascade of nested stages measured on the same denominator, all hypertensive adults (\cref{tab:cascade}).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Hypertension care cascade by WHO region, adults aged 30--79 years, 2019 (adapted from \cite{who2023hypertension}, Table~2).}\label{tab:cascade}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}X c c c c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Indicator} & \textbf{African Region} & \textbf{World} & \textbf{Americas} & \textbf{Europe} \\
\midrule
Age-standardised prevalence & 36\% & 33\% & 35\% & 37\% \\
Diagnosis coverage & 43\% & 54\% & 70\% & 66\% \\
Treatment coverage & 27\% & 42\% & 60\% & 53\% \\
Effective treatment (control) & 12\% & 21\% & 36\% & 26\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Two observations follow. First, the European Region has a higher prevalence than the African Region but a control rate more than twice as high: the African deficit is organisational rather than epidemiological. Second, the conditional losses derived from these figures are spread along the whole chain: in the African Region, 57\% of hypertensive adults are undiagnosed, 37\% of the diagnosed are untreated, and 56\% of the treated are not controlled. The largest conditional loss lies at the last link, among patients already in care. A screening device acts only on the first link; a longitudinal follow-up system acts on the second and third.

\subsection{The hypertension--diabetes comorbidity}\label{sec:comorbidity}

The association between hypertension and type~2 diabetes rests on shared risk factors---physical inactivity, unbalanced diet and obesity---and on a direct pathophysiological link: diabetes stiffens small vessels, which raises peripheral resistance and hence blood pressure, while hypertension accelerates the vascular damage of diabetes.

\Textcite{hinneh2023} produced the first systematic quantification of this comorbidity in Africa. Their meta-analysis, conducted according to PRISMA on PubMed, EMBASE and HINARI up to March 2023, pooled forty-one cross-sectional studies totalling 144\,433 participants. The pooled prevalence of hypertension among people with diabetes is 58.1\% (95\% CI 52.0--63.2), and 60.8\% among people with type~2 diabetes alone. Older age, overweight and obesity, longer duration of diabetes, urban residence and male sex are associated with a higher risk. The authors also note that patients with both conditions paradoxically have lower chances of receiving appropriate care before complications occur.

The sub-regional estimates place Central Africa highest (\cref{tab:subregional,fig:forest}).

\midrule

\paragraph{Reliability of the Central African estimate.}\label{sec:central-estimates} The value of 77.6\% must be reported with its confidence interval, 53.0--91.4\%, and it cannot by itself carry the justification of this work. It rests on two small Cameroonian hospital series (109 and 210 patients), while three other Central African studies listed in the article (1\,832 patients, prevalences of 35.0--50.4\%) do not appear in this subgroup. Heterogeneity is extreme ($I^2 \approx 98\%$), the included studies are mostly hospital-based and use varying diagnostic thresholds, and the restriction to English-language publications under-represents francophone Central Africa, as the authors themselves acknowledge. The figure also measures hypertension among people with diabetes, not the reverse.

The case for monitoring blood pressure and blood glucose jointly therefore rests on the robust continental estimate of 58.1\%, on the pathophysiological mechanism, and on the position of the WHO \parencite{who2023hypertension}: uncontrolled hypertension, not hyperglycaemia, is among the risk factors most strongly associated with premature death in people with diabetes; Member States have adopted a target of 80\% of people with diagnosed diabetes having good blood pressure control; and the HEARTS package developed for hypertension is explicitly applicable to diabetes and other long-term conditions.

\subsection{Comparability of estimates}\label{sec:comparability}

The prevalences quoted in this chapter are not directly comparable. They differ in reference population (general population, people with diabetes or hospital attendees), in diagnostic threshold (140/90 or 130/80~mmHg), in age structure, in the number of measurement visits---a single-visit measurement overestimates prevalence---and in recruitment mode, opportunistic screening and self-selection producing samples systematically different from probability sampling. None of the available sources is nationally representative for Cameroon. The nature of each estimate is therefore stated where it is used.

%##########################################################################
\section{Hypertension Care and Digital Health in Sub-Saharan Africa}\label{sec:lr-regional}\label{sec:diagnosis}

\subsection{Burden, determinants and care gaps}

\Textcite{gafane2024} synthesise the hypertension literature of sub-Saharan Africa published between January 2017 and June 2023, drawn from MEDLINE, PubMed, CINAHL, African Journals Online and grey literature. Most countries of the region reported prevalence data over the period, confirming a high burden with poor detection and control. The authors relate the rise of hypertension to the shift towards a westernised lifestyle---salt, sugar and fat, physical inactivity, obesity, tobacco and alcohol---together with socio-economic and psychosocial factors, and they identify human immunodeficiency virus infection as a regional specificity; among the mechanisms still under investigation they list sodium handling, endothelial function and circadian profiles such as nocturnal dipping. They distinguish individual obstacles to care (health literacy, cost, distance) from system obstacles (medicine availability, staffing and training, uneven political will). Community-based task shifting improves linkage to care and medication adherence, but its effect on blood pressure over time is inconsistent.

Their inventory of national surveys is the most recent available for Central Africa (\cref{tab:central-africa}). Dispersion is wide, from 13.5\% to 42.1\%, and reflects the diversity of instruments as much as real differences; three countries of the sub-region have no data for the period. In Cameroon, the May Measurement Month campaign of 2019 found a prevalence of 20.8\%, with only 11.2\% of hypertensive participants controlled.

\toprule

\midrule

\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The review is narrative rather than pooled, the surveys it gathers are heterogeneous, and some figures are inconsistent: the same pair of values (48\% and 34\%) is presented once as prevalence and once as diagnosis rate. It is used here for the regional profile and the care cascade, not for pooled prevalence.

\subsection{Why the episodic care model fails}\label{sec:ltfu}\label{sec:inertia}\label{sec:time}\label{sec:records}

The WHO documents the mechanisms behind the failure of the cascade \parencite{who2023hypertension}. \emph{Loss to follow-up} often affects half of patients or more in resource-limited settings; the report therefore holds that screening is useful only if linked to effective follow-up, and it recommends opportunistic detection in health facilities together with retention of identified patients rather than population screening. \emph{Therapeutic inertia}, the tendency not to intensify treatment despite uncontrolled blood pressure, explains why the last link of the cascade is the weakest; a simple standardised protocol helps reduce it. Lifestyle modification alone lowers systolic pressure by only about 1.3~mmHg in primary-care trials, so almost all diagnosed patients need medication, and its timely adjustment. \emph{Consultation time} is often below five minutes in primary care, which requires digital tools to be fast and to replace paper rather than add to it. Finally, \emph{paper records} are duplicated, lost and unavailable during the visit, whereas a secure longitudinal record is described as essential for hypertension, diabetes and other long-term conditions. The core indicators of the HEARTS programme, two of which are temporal, define what such a record must produce (\cref{sec:hearts-spec}).

The expected follow-up frequency is set by Recommendation~7 of the WHO guideline on the pharmacological treatment of hypertension: monthly follow-up after initiating or changing treatment until the target is reached, then every three to six months once blood pressure is controlled \parencite{who2021guideline}. The HEARTS treatment-protocol module adds that the return interval should usually be four weeks or less during titration \parencite{whoHEARTS}. The gap between this requirement and the one or two contacts a year that an episodic model delivers is quantified in \cref{sec:followup-requires}.

The programmes cited by the WHO point in the same direction. In Sri Lanka, 86\% of enrolled patients return every three months and 57\% are controlled; in the Philippines, India and Cuba, part of the follow-up was moved out of the facility, towards the home or the community. These are associations observed in programmes that changed several components at once, not causal estimates. The Indian programme also shows that retention depends on medicine supply: stock-outs soon after launch discouraged patients from returning, so that a telemonitoring system deployed during stock-outs would probably not improve control, whatever its technical performance.

\subsection{Telemedicine evidence from Cameroon}\label{sec:telemedcam}\label{sec:bediang}\label{sec:cameroon-joint}\label{sec:specialist-access}

Two controlled evaluations of cardiovascular telemedicine have been conducted in Cameroonian primary care.

\paragraph{TELEMED-CAM (2008).} \Textcite{kingue2013} evaluated a hypertension management package in thirty rural health centres supported by a telemedicine centre at Yaoundé General Hospital. The package combined a standardised protocol restricted to generic drugs, lifestyle counselling, training of general practitioners and nurses, and a telecare channel through which providers reported patient data daily by text or voice message and received telephone feedback. Among 268 participants followed for 24 weeks, attendance at scheduled visits was higher in the intervention arm at every grade of hypertension ($p \leq 0.04$), and systolic pressure fell from the second visit. The benefit on control was confined to stage~III hypertension (no improvement in 16.7\% against 33.3\%, $p = 0.04$); for stages I and II the proportions at target were similar (65.2\% against 70\%, $p = 0.20$). Allocation was not strictly random, clustering was ignored, the arms differed at baseline (systolic pressure 169 against 161~mmHg, diabetes 9.1\% against 43.6\%), and telecare was only one component of the package, so its own effect cannot be isolated. The study also quantifies the cost of systematic remote review: about six calls per provider per day, of about five minutes each.

\paragraph{Tele-expertise in district hospitals (2016--2017).} \Textcite{bediang2021} compared two district hospitals equipped with a twelve-lead electrocardiograph and an internet tele-expertise platform (Mbouda and Akonolinga) with two matched control hospitals (Foumbot and Sa'a). Among 171 patients, access to an electrocardiogram with a cardiologist's opinion reached 98.9\% against 33.3\% (relative risk 2.97), the expert modified the local provider's diagnosis in 46.2\% of cases against 17.9\%, and patient satisfaction was higher. The study was not randomised and the groups differed at baseline; its primary outcome measures the availability of the service rather than its clinical effect.\label{sec:bediang-appraisal} The reported blood pressure benefit (40.9\% against 17.9\% of patients with a favourable evolution) counts the 52 control patients who never returned as not improved; restricted to the patients actually reviewed at three months, the proportions become 41.3\% against 53.8\%. The specialist's opinion also took 10.3 days on average against a protocol maximum of 72 hours, no faster than in the control centres (11.2 days, $p = 0.82$): the bottleneck was expert availability, not transmission.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The two Cameroonian evaluations compared.}\label{tab:cameroon-pair}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.6cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{\textcite{kingue2013}} & \textbf{\textcite{bediang2021}} \\
\midrule
Period & 2008 & 2016--2017 \\
Function evaluated & telemanagement of treatment & tele-expertise for diagnosis \\
Design & prospective, partly randomised by centre & controlled, matched centres, not randomised \\
Participants & 268 & 171 \\
Technology & GSM handset, SMS and voicemail & portable 12-lead ECG, web platform \\
Principal result & control improved in stage III only & access to ECG with expertise, 98.9\% vs 33.3\% \\
Retention result & visit adherence higher at every grade & attendance at 3 months, 98.9\% vs 33.3\% \\
Main weakness & clustering ignored, package effect & non-comparable groups, differential attrition \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Read together (\cref{tab:cameroon-pair}), the two studies support three conclusions. Retention is the most robust result, observed by different mechanisms eight years apart. An effect on blood pressure control is not established in Cameroon. And the value added lies in the clinical decision---a revised diagnosis, a supported prescriber---rather than in a tool placed in the patient's hands. Both studies also show that systematic expert review of every case does not scale where specialists are scarce.

\subsection{Digital health landscape and barriers to adoption}\label{sec:digital-barriers}\label{sec:regional-landscape}

\Textcite{dodoo2022} reviewed sixty-six empirical studies and government reports on telemedicine in sub-Saharan Africa, drawn from eight databases under adapted PRISMA guidelines. Progress is markedly uneven between countries, and the barriers fall into six dimensions (\cref{tab:barriers}). Two findings bear directly on design: most projects lost momentum once external funding ended, and health professionals facing a heavy workload perceive telemedicine as an additional task. The authors also report that store-and-forward services suffered from low user satisfaction for lack of real-time interaction. Their inventory lists for Cameroon telecardiology, wound-care and tele-education services, including the Telemed-cam project, and they record forty-one countries with national digital health strategies. Francophone countries, Cameroon included, are under-represented in the literature they review.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Six dimensions of barriers to telemedicine in sub-Saharan Africa \parencite{dodoo2022}.}\label{tab:barriers}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.4cm} X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Dimension} & \textbf{Definition given by the authors} \\
\midrule
Technological & information systems and connectivity \\
Organisational & stakeholder engagement, sustainability of systems \\
Individual & users' experience, knowledge and awareness \\
Financial & financial resources and operating cost \\
Cultural & social constraints and traditional beliefs \\
Legal and regulatory & policies, legislation, confidentiality and privacy protection \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\Textcite{agbeyangi2025} extend this picture with a PRISMA-guided systematic review of fifty-three studies published between 2014 and 2024, comparing South Africa, Kenya and Nigeria. South Africa leads adoption, including specialist teleconsultation and chronic disease management; Kenya shows strong integration of mobile health; Nigeria is advancing remote diagnosis under infrastructural constraints. Regulatory gaps, digital literacy and infrastructure remain the persistent barriers. Central Africa is absent from the comparison.

\Textcite{segun2025} assess the digital health landscape of the region from the perspective of cardiovascular care. More than 80\% of the population holds a mobile telephone subscription, mobile internet penetration stood at 25\% in 2023, and smartphone adoption is projected to reach 88\% by 2030; yet about 550 million people lack reliable access to electricity and the internet, and up to 18\% of countries had no registered cardiologist in 2020. The collapse of the Rwandan platform Babyl in 2023, after about 2.5 million users had registered, illustrates that adoption is not sustainability. Artificial intelligence remains largely untested in the region, with few validation studies in African populations and a documented risk that externally trained algorithms perform poorly there. As a perspective article without a declared search strategy, this source is used for contextual orders of magnitude, not as pooled evidence.

Taken together, these sources describe a population that is reachable by mobile networks and a specialist who is not. A system that forwards every reading to a clinician reproduces the scarcity it aims to relieve; a system that sorts readings and orders the clinician's attention addresses it. They also impose offline operation, a simple interface for both patients and providers, and a documented operating cost.

%##########################################################################
\section{Mobile Health Self-Management and Clinical Evidence}\label{sec:outpatient}\label{sec:evidence}

\subsection{What self-management applications offer}\label{sec:apps}\label{sec:bct}

\Textcite{alessa2019} analysed the 186 hypertension self-management applications available in English on the two main application stores in January 2018, coding thirty of them in depth. Most applications offered a narrow set of functions: 58.1\% offered a single function, only 17.7\% offered three or more, and none offered all eight identified. Self-monitoring was present in 53.2\% of applications, feedback in 28.0\% and reminders in 21.0\%; most of the applications that tracked blood pressure required the patient to type in the value displayed by the monitor. None of the thirty applications analysed in depth had been scientifically evaluated or certified as a medical device, and none reported the involvement of patients in its design. Three applications claimed to measure blood pressure with the smartphone camera without any evidence of validity. Of the thirty, only twenty had a privacy policy, and fourteen of these declared sharing data with third parties. In terms of behaviour change techniques, coded with the taxonomy of Michie and colleagues, the applications contained a median of nine techniques, with self-monitoring, prompts and cues, and action planning present in all thirty. As a content analysis of a market snapshot, the study describes what applications contain, not whether they work.

\subsection{Pooled clinical effects}\label{sec:efficacy}\label{sec:oversight}\label{sec:evidence-deficit}

\Textcite{mao2020} pooled fifty-one randomised trials published between 2008 and 2019, with 13\,054 participants with diabetes or hypertension. Mobile health interventions reduced glycated haemoglobin (HbA1c) by 0.39 points ($-0.50$ to $-0.29$), fasting glucose by 0.52~mmol/L, systolic pressure by 2.99~mmHg ($-4.19$ to $-1.80$) and diastolic pressure by 1.14~mmHg. Two subgroup results are decisive for this work. Interventions combining mobile health with professional management reduced systolic pressure by 6.17~mmHg ($-8.83$ to $-3.50$), whereas mobile health alone produced a non-significant 2.16~mmHg. And the effect on systolic pressure was present in developed countries ($-5.72$~mmHg) but absent in developing countries ($+0.25$~mmHg, $-3.10$ to $3.59$), whose trials used mobile health without professional management; the glycaemic effect, by contrast, was larger in developing countries ($-0.52$ against $-0.35$ points). Only one of the fifty-one trials was conducted in sub-Saharan Africa. Heterogeneity was moderate to high ($I^2$ of 57--67\%) and most trials were short.

\Textcite{zhou2024} focused on mobile application self-management for adults with hypertension: twenty randomised trials published from 2017 onwards, sixteen of them meta-analysed, with 4\,168 participants from nine countries, none in sub-Saharan Africa. Systolic pressure fell by 5.78~mmHg ($-7.97$ to $-3.59$; low certainty, $I^2 = 82\%$) and diastolic pressure by 3.28~mmHg (moderate certainty). The subgroup analyses link the effect to the design of the intervention (\cref{tab:subgroups}): interventions with at least eleven behaviour change techniques and those addressing several risk factors were markedly more effective, and three-month interventions outperformed longer ones, which the authors attribute to the weakening of habit after about twelve weeks. Fifteen of the twenty trials were at high risk of bias, and interventions based mainly on text messages or calls---the type most often evaluated in Africa---were excluded.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small

\toprule
\textbf{Factor} & \textbf{Modality} & \textbf{Effect on SBP (95\% CI)} & \textbf{$P$} \\
\midrule
Risk factors addressed & multiple & $-6.50$~mmHg ($-9.00$ to $-3.99$) & .007 \\
                       & single   & $-1.54$~mmHg ($-4.15$ to $1.06$) & \\
\addlinespace
Number of techniques & $\geq$ 11 BCT & $-9.68$~mmHg ($-13.49$ to $-5.87$) & $<$.001 \\
                     & $<$ 11 BCT    & $-2.88$~mmHg ($-3.90$ to $-1.86$) & \\
\addlinespace

\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\Textcite{bandeira2024} updated the synthesis of randomised trials of mobile health interventions on blood pressure. They confirm statistically and clinically significant reductions across diverse populations and settings, and identify telemonitoring with clinician feedback as the most effective modality. Heterogeneity between interventions, short follow-up in several trials and the scarcity of data from low- and middle-income countries limit the generalisation of these results.%
\verify{Extract the pooled systolic and diastolic estimates and the subgroup estimate for telemonitoring with clinician feedback from \textcite{bandeira2024}, and add them to \cref{tab:effect-synthesis}.}

\subsection{A randomised trial in the target population}\label{sec:mihevc}\label{sec:mechanism}\label{sec:feasibility}

\Textcite{mihevc2025} conducted the trial most directly relevant to this work: a multicentre, open-label randomised trial in three Slovenian primary-care centres between 2021 and 2023, enrolling 128 patients aged 65 years or more with both hypertension and type~2 diabetes. In addition to usual care, the intervention group measured blood pressure and blood glucose at home with Bluetooth devices that transmitted automatically through a smartphone to a cloud platform; a telemedicine centre coordinated by a general practitioner reviewed the values according to a three-level protocol (green, yellow, red) detailed in \cref{sec:triage}. After twelve months, systolic pressure was 6.9~mmHg lower than in the control group ($-11$ to $-2.7$), after a difference of 10.5~mmHg at six months, and HbA1c was 0.5 points lower.%
\verify{The four HbA1c means reported by \textcite{mihevc2025} (7.2 to 6.7 and 7.1 to 6.7) imply a difference in change of $-0.1$, not the reported $-0.5$ ($-0.8$ to $-0.2$). Check Table~3 of the article before citing the glycaemic result.}

Three further results matter. Telemonitoring had no effect on other cardiovascular risk factors, on diabetes-related quality of life or on lifestyle: the authors attribute the clinical effect to improved communication, medication adherence, early detection of exacerbations and treatment adjustment based on accurate data, not to behaviour change. Seven of the 64 patients of the intervention arm (10.9\%) withdrew in the first month because they could not use the equipment, despite one hour of individual training. And about a third of the systolic effect was lost between six and twelve months. The trial was conducted in a high-income setting, among older adults, without blinding; its applicability to low-resource settings requires adaptation.

\subsection{Synthesis of the clinical evidence}\label{sec:consolidated}

\Cref{tab:effect-synthesis,fig:sbp-forest} gather the available estimates in chronological order.

Four conclusions emerge. First, mobile health and telemonitoring produce a modest but clinically relevant effect, of the order of $-3$ to $-7$~mmHg on systolic pressure and $-0.4$ to $-0.5$ points on HbA1c; these are intermediate outcomes, and none of the sources measures morbidity or mortality. Second, the effect depends on professional oversight: \textcite{mao2020}, \textcite{bandeira2024} and \textcite{mihevc2025} converge on this point by independent routes, and so do the Cameroonian studies of \cref{sec:telemedcam}. Third, the evidence from sub-Saharan Africa is almost absent, and the absence of effect observed in developing countries is plausibly a design gap---no clinician at the end of the chain---rather than a context effect. Fourth, two problems remain open: the erosion of the effect over time, and the inability of a fraction of patients to use the equipment. Telemonitoring must therefore be built as a decision-support system linked to a clinician, not as a self-tracking tool handed to the patient.

%##########################################################################
\section{Sensor and IoT Technologies for Chronic Disease Monitoring}\label{sec:lr-iot}

\subsection{The normative position on measuring devices}\label{sec:metrology}

The WHO sets the frame within which any sensor must be assessed \parencite{who2023hypertension}. Manual devices should be replaced by independently validated automated electronic devices, and automated upper-arm cuff devices are recommended. Cuffless devices, including popular connected wristbands, are not recommended for the management of hypertension, because of unresolved questions about their accuracy and their ability to track blood pressure change; as of 2023, no alternative to the validated upper-arm cuff is accurate enough for diagnosis and management. Only about one automated device in five on the market has evidence of validation.

\subsection{Non-invasive sensing of glucose and blood pressure}\label{sec:cuffless}

\Textcite{alhaddad2022} reviewed the non-invasive sensing modalities proposed for blood glucose---electrocardiography, electromagnetic sensing, bioimpedance and photoplethysmography (PPG)---and the machine learning models applied to them. PPG- and ECG-based sensing combined with deep learning shows promise for detecting glucose trends and hypoglycaemia, but not for clinical-grade absolute measurement: many studies have small samples, inter-individual variation limits generalisation, and accuracy remains below the standards recognised by the Food and Drug Administration.

\Textcite{liu2025ppg} estimated blood pressure from the PPG signal recorded by a smartphone camera alone, without additional hardware. Combining waveform feature engineering with machine learning, and using SHAP values to trace each prediction back to interpretable physiological features, they report a strong correlation with reference devices and assess agreement by Bland--Altman analysis on 127 participants. The sample is modest and single-site, accuracy remains below that of clinical cuff devices, and generalisation across skin tones, devices and lighting conditions is not established.

These two studies, set against the WHO position and the unvalidated camera applications found by \textcite{alessa2019}, define a consistent position. Blood glucose must come from a glucometer and blood pressure from a validated upper-arm cuff, because these are the measurements on which clinical decisions rest. Smartphone PPG is a plausible low-cost source of heart rate and an exploratory source of blood pressure trends, to be evaluated rather than relied upon (\cref{sec:contradiction}).

\subsection{IoT architectures for remote monitoring}

\Textcite{tai2024} address the difficulty of applying conventional machine learning to noisy, heterogeneous and high-dimensional IoT health data. They propose a human-centric deep learning technique for remote chronic disease monitoring, embedded in a standardised three-phase architecture: data acquisition, data processing and data visualisation. The architecture supports accurate and interpretable predictions in their evaluation, which was conducted in controlled conditions and does not address the connectivity constraints of low-resource settings.%
\verify{Only the bibliographic record and abstract-level summary of \textcite{tai2024} were used. Check the evaluation dataset and reported performance against the full text.}

\Textcite{efendi2025} describe and validate a complete IoT system for monitoring elderly people. Its three layers are a physical layer (a MAX30100 optical sensor for heart rate and oxygen saturation and a MAX30205 temperature sensor, read by an ESP32 microcontroller), a network layer (MQTT transmission to a Firebase real-time database) and an application layer (mobile application and website accessed over HTTP). Firebase was chosen for real-time synchronisation, built-in authentication, and local persistence with automatic merging when connectivity returns. Against a digital oximeter and thermometer, six participants measured for ten minutes at rest gave a mean absolute percentage error of 0.90\%, and XGBoost was the best of five classifiers of the user's state as stable or not stable. The system measures heart rate, oxygen saturation and temperature, not blood pressure or blood glucose; its validation sample is small, the reference instruments are not identified as validated, and its classifier is examined critically in \cref{sec:lr-ml}.

\Textcite{liu2025review} survey the use of IoT mobile sensing and machine learning for cardiovascular disease, cancer, chronic respiratory disease and diabetes. They classify devices into portable, wearable, intelligent clinical, remote, implantable and ingestible categories, describe a seven-layer platform from device access to decision support, and identify the weaknesses of traditional monitoring: manual processes, information silos, lack of real-time feedback and of early-warning mechanisms. They also note that electronic medical records require cleaning of duplicates, unit errors and values carried over identically between visits. Although presented as a systematic review, the article reports no protocol, flow diagram or number of included studies, so it is treated here as a narrative survey. Controlled settings dominate the studies it covers, and deployment in low- and middle-income countries is poorly represented.

The architectures converge on a common structure: acquisition, transmission and storage, processing, and presentation. None of them has been validated on real patients in routine care in a low-resource setting, and none acquires blood pressure and blood glucose through its own sensors. The detailed implications for acquisition and architecture are developed in \cref{sec:acquisition,sec:cloud}.

%##########################################################################
\section{Machine Learning for Clinical Risk Prediction}\label{sec:lr-ml}

\Textcite{chatrati2022} proposed a smart home monitoring system that analyses home blood pressure and glucose readings and notifies the healthcare provider when an abnormality is detected, combining conditional rules for hypertension with a learned classifier for diabetes. It is the closest published precedent of a system combining both measurements with machine learning. As described, however, it classifies single readings rather than series, and it has not been evaluated on patients in routine care.%
\verify{Only the abstract of \textcite{chatrati2022} was consulted. Check the dataset, the rule thresholds, the reported performance and whether readings or windows are modelled.}

\Textcite{liu2025review} report that artificial neural networks, support vector machines, random forests, gradient boosting and deep learning are the dominant algorithm families for chronic disease monitoring. The accuracies they collect cluster between 95\% and 100\%, whereas the only recurrent network applied to a genuine clinical prediction task, the prediction of readmission in lupus, reaches an area under the curve (AUC) of 0.70. They name the black-box nature of these models and their dependence on large labelled datasets as their main limitations.

The classifier comparison of \textcite{efendi2025} illustrates why such figures must be read with care. XGBoost reached an accuracy of 0.973 and the four other models exceeded 0.92. However, the label \emph{stable} is defined by the same normal ranges that are used to discretise the three input features, so the label is a deterministic function of the inputs: the models recover a rule rather than predict a state. Some reported metrics are also mutually inconsistent, and the training configuration described (epochs, sigmoid activation, Adam optimiser) does not apply to tree-based models. The result does not show that XGBoost outperforms other algorithms (\cref{sec:task-not-algo}).

\Textcite{mendoza2025} provide the counter-example of a genuine prediction task. Using 1\,308 hypertensive adults from the nationally representative Mexican survey ENSANUT~2022, they predicted uncontrolled blood pressure (130/80~mmHg threshold, prevalence 30.3\%) from seven predictors selected by LASSO: sex, age, socio-economic status, years since diagnosis, history of diabetes, source of medical care and adherence to a nutritional plan. Random forest reached an AUC of 0.75 (0.72--0.77), logistic regression 0.61 and XGBoost 0.54 (0.49--0.60), the last being indistinguishable from chance. Variable importance and SHAP values ranked age and years since diagnosis first, and the data and code are public. The best model nevertheless detects only 51\% of uncontrolled cases (specificity 0.98); the logistic regression row is computed with a different positive class from the other two, and the discussion attributes to random forest a sensitivity that is in fact its specificity (\cref{sec:metrics}). The design is cross-sectional and the predictors are demographic and administrative rather than physiological.

Three lessons follow. Reported performance is governed by how the task is constructed---label integrity and informativeness of the features---far more than by the choice of algorithm. Ensemble tree models, random forest and XGBoost, are plausible candidates, and interpretability through feature importance and SHAP is now expected practice, as \textcite{mendoza2025} and \textcite{liu2025ppg} both show. And no source forecasts a future physiological state from a patient's own series of blood pressure and blood glucose readings, nor evaluates such a model in a low-resource setting. The methodological consequences are developed in \cref{sec:ml}.

%##########################################################################
\section{Data Privacy, Security and Ethics in Digital Health}\label{sec:lr-privacy}

\Textcite{alhammad2024} conducted the first systematic review of patients' perspectives on the confidentiality, privacy and security of the data collected by mobile health applications: thirty-three studies published between 2014 and 2022, identified in PubMed, Ovid and ScienceDirect under PRISMA guidance. Patients' concerns about their data are a consistent and significant barrier to adoption. Encryption, secure authentication and regular risk assessment are recommended as baseline safeguards, and transparency about how data are handled improves trust. Most included studies come from high-income countries and measure self-reported perceptions rather than actual incidents.

These findings converge with earlier observations of this review: most commercial applications either lack a privacy policy or declare sharing data with third parties \parencite{alessa2019}, the legal and regulatory dimension is one of the six barriers to telemedicine in the region \parencite{dodoo2022}, and the reference implementation of \textcite{efendi2025} stores its data in a cloud region outside the country of use, which raises the question of data residency. Since the present work processes real, hospital-sourced patient data through a mobile and cloud pipeline, data protection is treated as a first-class design constraint: anonymisation before modelling, encrypted transmission, role-based authentication, and explicit identification of the applicable Cameroonian legal framework (\cref{sec:security,sec:legal}).

%##########################################################################
\section{Synthesis and Research Gap}\label{sec:positioning}

\Cref{tab:lr-synthesis} summarises, for each theme, what the literature establishes, what it leaves open, and the consequence for this work.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Synthesis of the literature review.}\label{tab:lr-synthesis}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.3cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Theme} & \textbf{Established} & \textbf{Not established} & \textbf{Consequence for this work} \\
\midrule
Burden & high burden; cascade fails at every stage, most at control; 58\% of people with diabetes are hypertensive & reliable Central African and Cameroonian estimates & longitudinal follow-up of diagnosed patients, joint BP and glucose monitoring \\
Sub-Saharan context & loss to follow-up, inertia, paper records; six barrier dimensions; specialist scarcity & BP effect of telemedicine in Cameroon; evidence from Central Africa & offline-first, low workload, triage of clinician attention \\
Clinical evidence & modest effect on BP and HbA1c, conditional on professional oversight & effectiveness in sub-Saharan Africa; long-term effect & clinician-in-the-loop decision support \\
Sensors and IoT & validated cuff and glucometer required; layered architectures with offline storage & validated cuffless BP; validation on real patients in low-resource settings & instruments for BP and glucose, smartphone sensing exploratory \\
Machine learning & ensemble trees plausible; interpretability expected; task construction drives performance & forecasting from individual BP and glucose series & leakage-free forecasting formulation, honest metrics \\
Privacy & data concerns hinder adoption; baseline safeguards known & evidence from African patients & anonymisation, encryption, declared data residency \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The research gap can therefore be stated precisely. The need for longitudinal follow-up is established, and telemonitoring linked to a clinician improves blood pressure and glycaemic control in high-income settings, including in older patients with both conditions \parencite{mihevc2025}. The only validated protocol for this population, however, relies on fixed thresholds applied uniformly to all patients, triggers on the level of a reading rather than on its trend, and uses no learned model. No evaluated system combines consultation data, home measurements and mobile sensor data for hypertension and diabetes; none applies machine learning to the longitudinal series of the individual patient to anticipate deterioration and to order the clinician's attention; and none has been evaluated in Central Africa, under the connectivity, workload, sustainability and data-protection constraints documented in this chapter. The present work addresses this gap. \Cref{ch:techsota} derives from the review the requirements that such a system must meet.


%==========================================================================
% PART II
%==========================================================================
\part{Methodology, Modelling and System Architecture Design}

%==========================================================================
% CHAPTER 3 -- DESIGN REQUIREMENTS
%==========================================================================
\chapter{Design Requirements Derived from the Literature}\label{ch:techsota}

\Cref{ch:background} established what is known and what is missing. This chapter turns those findings into requirements for the proposed system. It proceeds from the clinical requirements of measurement and follow-up (\cref{sec:clinical-req}) to the acquisition of the data (\cref{sec:acquisition}), its transport and storage (\cref{sec:cloud}), its modelling (\cref{sec:ml}) and the generation of alerts (\cref{sec:cdss}). Each section closes with a specification table that states every requirement together with its basis. Beyond the sources of the review, two reference architectures for chronic disease telemonitoring are used here as engineering references \parencite{yazdani2018,mucchi2021}, and the research framing document of this work is confronted with the evidence wherever it states a design choice.

%##########################################################################
\section{Clinical Requirements for Measurement and Follow-up}\label{sec:clinical-req}\label{sec:protocol-gap}

The review showed that the episodic care model fails because it produces point-in-time information, whereas diagnosis, treatment adjustment and programme monitoring all require repeated measurements organised as a series (\cref{sec:ltfu}). This section translates that finding into measurement and follow-up requirements.

\subsection{Diagnosis and the variability of blood pressure}

\subsubsection{What diagnosis requires}\label{sec:dx-requires}

The diagnostic protocol recommended by the WHO is demanding, and its detail is worth reporting, because the insufficiency of the episodic model follows from it.

Measurement must be performed by trained staff, in a standardised way, with a cuff of appropriate size, the patient seated comfortably, back supported, bladder empty and legs uncrossed. Two readings must be taken at the first encounter, and the result of the second guides the decision whether to schedule a confirmation visit. What follows depends on the level observed (\cref{tab:protocol}).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Recommended action by confirmed pressure level at the first encounter \parencite{who2023hypertension}.}\label{tab:protocol}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{6.2cm} X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Confirmed pressure on both readings of the day} & \textbf{Recommended action} \\
\midrule
SBP $\geq$ 160 or DBP $\geq$ 100~mmHg & start treatment the same day \\
SBP 140--159 or DBP 90--99~mmHg & confirm at an additional visit, usually one to four weeks later \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Diagnosis is made only if, at both visits, systolic pressure is $\geq 140$~mmHg and/or diastolic pressure is $\geq 90$~mmHg.

Diagnosis is therefore, by construction, a longitudinal act: it requires two measurement points separated by one to four weeks (the recheck window already noted in \cref{sec:cascade}). A care model that does not guarantee the patient's return for the second visit does not produce imperfect diagnoses; it produces none.

\subsubsection{What variability imposes}\label{sec:variability}

Blood pressure is a highly dynamic vital sign, influenced by time of day, activity, stress and the measurement environment. The WHO observes accordingly that, in population screening, many people with elevated pressure no longer meet the diagnostic criteria when assessed more carefully at follow-up \parencite{who2023hypertension}.

This variability can be formalised. For patient $i$ measured at time $t$,
\begin{equation}
  b_{i,t} = \mu_i + \varepsilon_{i,t},
\end{equation}
where $\mu_i$ is the patient's usual pressure, the only clinically relevant quantity, and $\varepsilon_{i,t}$ gathers circadian variability, variability due to activity and stress, the effect of the measurement environment and instrument error. A single measurement estimates $\mu_i$ with a variance equal to that of $\varepsilon$; $n$ sufficiently spaced measurements, whose errors are independent, reduce it by a factor $n$:
\begin{equation}
  \operatorname{Var}(\hat\mu_i) = \frac{\sigma_\varepsilon^2}{n}.
\end{equation}
Independence is an assumption: two readings taken in the same sitting share their context, so the gain from the second is smaller than the formula suggests, which is one more reason why the protocol also asks for a second visit.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=0.8\textwidth,height=6cm,
  xlabel={Number of independent measurements $n$},
  ylabel={$\operatorname{Var}(\hat\mu_i)/\sigma_\varepsilon^2$},
  xmin=0,xmax=34,ymin=0,ymax=1.08,grid=major,grid style={gray!20}]
\addplot[thick,cRef,domain=1:34,samples=100]{1/x};
\addplot[only marks,mark=*,mark size=2.5pt,cAfrica,nodes near coords,point meta=explicit symbolic,
  every node near coord/.append style={font=\footnotesize,anchor=south west}]
  coordinates {(2,0.5) [two visits] (4,0.25) [four visits] (30,0.033) [30 daily readings]};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Relative variance of the estimate of the usual pressure $\mu_i$ as a function of the number of independent measurements. Errors are assumed independent.}\label{fig:variance}
\end{figure}

This is the logic of the two-reading, two-visit protocol (\cref{fig:variance}). It is also, and this is the central argument of this thesis, the logic that favours home measurement: where a consultation provides at best two to four points per year, home follow-up produces several dozen, which reduces uncertainty about $\mu_i$ and additionally allows $\sigma_\varepsilon$ itself, the patient's blood pressure variability, to be estimated, an independent risk factor.

\subsection{Follow-up frequency}

\subsubsection{What follow-up requires}\label{sec:followup-requires}

Recommendation~7 of the WHO 2021 guideline on the pharmacological treatment of hypertension \parencite{who2021guideline} sets the expected follow-up frequency:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item monthly follow-up after initiating or changing antihypertensive treatment, until the patient reaches the blood pressure target;
  \item follow-up every three to six months for patients whose pressure is controlled.
\end{itemize}
The therapeutic protocol module of the HEARTS technical package \parencite{whoHEARTS} further specifies that a simple protocol must set the return-visit interval, \bq{usually four weeks or less}, to allow rapid titration of doses and drugs until control is reached.

Let us confront this requirement with the data of \cref{sec:burden}. In the African Region, treatment coverage is 27\% and control 12\%, from which we derived that 56\% of treated patients do not reach the target (\cref{sec:cascade}). According to recommendation~7, these patients should be seen monthly until they do.

The gap can be quantified (\cref{tab:contacts}). An uncontrolled patient should receive about twelve contacts per year, a controlled patient two to four. For an active list in which a share $\pi_u = 0.56$ of patients is uncontrolled, the theoretical need is
\begin{equation}
  \bar n = \pi_u \times 12 + (1-\pi_u)\times n_c \approx 7.6 \text{ to } 8.5 \text{ contacts per patient per year}, \qquad n_c \in [2,4].
\end{equation}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Follow-up contacts per patient per year required by WHO recommendation~7.}\label{tab:contacts}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}X c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Patient profile} & \textbf{Contacts per year} \\
\midrule
Uncontrolled: monthly follow-up until the target is reached & 12 \\
Controlled: follow-up every three to six months & 2 to 4 \\
Active list with 56\% uncontrolled (\cref{sec:cascade}) & 7.6 to 8.5 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

If the episodic model delivers one to two contacts per year, it delivers between about an eighth and about a quarter of the recommended frequency.%
\verify{The figure of one to two delivered contacts per year has no source in the draft, where it was introduced as ``available data for the region''. Cite a regional or Cameroonian source, or present it explicitly as an illustrative scenario. The draft's ``one fifth to one quarter'' assumed half of the list uncontrolled; with the 56\% derived in \cref{sec:cascade} the range is about 12--26\%.}
It is this cumulative gap, and not the duration of each consultation taken in isolation, that constitutes the structural deficit.

\subsection{From act data to series data}\label{sec:act-series}

The longitudinal record that the WHO describes as essential (\cref{sec:records}) matters because clinical information only acquires its meaning in a series.


This distinction between act data and series data is central to the system design (\cref{fig:act-series}). A pressure of 152/94~mmHg does not have the same meaning depending on whether it follows a stable series around 135/85 or is part of a progressive drift observed over three months. The first case calls for a check, the second for therapeutic intensification. The paper register cannot distinguish the two situations; from an informational viewpoint, this is the reason why therapeutic inertia persists.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=0.9\textwidth,height=6.2cm,xlabel={Week},ylabel={Systolic pressure (mmHg)},
  xmin=0,xmax=11,ymin=125,ymax=158,xtick={0,2,...,10},grid=major,grid style={gray!20},
  legend pos=north west,legend style={font=\footnotesize}]
\addplot[thick,cRef,mark=*] coordinates {(0,136)(1,134)(2,137)(3,135)(4,133)(5,136)(6,135)(7,134)(8,137)(9,135)(10,136)(11,152)};
\addplot[thick,cAfrica,mark=square*] coordinates {(0,131)(1,133)(2,132)(3,136)(4,137)(5,140)(6,141)(7,144)(8,146)(9,148)(10,150)(11,152)};
\addplot[dashed,cGrey,no markers,domain=0:11]{140};
\legend{Stable series with isolated excursion,Progressive drift,Threshold (140~mmHg)};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Same last value, different meaning. Schematic illustration with synthetic values, not patient data: a single reading of 152~mmHg is a check-up case in one series and an intensification case in the other.}\label{fig:act-series}
\end{figure}

A further requirement follows. Information silos are not only a consequence of paper: they re-form in a digital environment as soon as each device produces its own format \parencite{liu2025review}. The present work must therefore define a single, documented data schema, rather than aggregating heterogeneous flows after the fact.


\textcite{liu2025review} note that electronic medical records themselves require prior cleaning, the typical artefacts being duplicates, unit errors and values carried over identically from one visit to the next. This must be kept for the experimental part: digitisation does not remove the data-quality problem, it displaces it. Any model trained on real consultation data must include an explicit step to detect these three artefacts, and the identically carried-over value, which falsely simulates clinical stability, is the most dangerous of the three for a trend-detection model.

\subsubsection{What the WHO expects from an information system}\label{sec:hearts-spec}

The report does not stop at the finding: it defines the expected outputs of a hypertension information system, as a short list of core HEARTS indicators. This list is a directly usable functional specification (\cref{tab:hearts}).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Core HEARTS programme indicators \parencite{who2023hypertension}.}\label{tab:hearts}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.6cm} X c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Indicator} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Temporal} \\
\midrule
Programme coverage & number of hypertensive patients registered in the catchment area & no \\
Blood pressure control & proportion of registered patients with BP $<$ 140/90~mmHg over the last three months & yes \\
Retention in care & proportion of registered patients with no visit in the last three months, and in the last twelve months & yes \\
Availability & availability of essential antihypertensive medicines and BP measurement devices & no \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Two observations are in order. First, two of the four core indicators are temporal: they can only be computed from a dated history, and a paper register cannot produce them except at the cost of an impracticable manual review. Second, the control indicator is itself defined over a three-month sliding window, which confirms that the quantity of interest is a property of the series and not of a single measurement.

The WHO specifies that when programme managers receive these indicators monthly, they can identify lagging facilities and implement targeted improvements. It cites the DHIS2 Hypertension Tracker as an example of a user-friendly mobile application. Among the six maturity domains conditioning the national scale-up of a programme, it also includes the presence of a digital information system for hypertension management, with, as a clinical quality factor, a missed-visit rate below 25\%, defined as failure to return to a scheduled visit within the preceding three or six months. The other five domains are clinical capacity, service delivery, clinical quality, medicine supply and political commitment.

\subsection{Self-measurement protocols}\label{sec:protocols}

\subsubsection{Blood pressure self-measurement}

The WHO, whose protocol we reported in \cref{sec:dx-requires}, sets the conditions of measurement: trained staff or patient, cuff of appropriate size, seated position with back supported, empty bladder, legs uncrossed, two readings of which the second is retained. It further establishes that only independently validated automated electronic upper-arm cuff devices are recommended, and that no cuffless alternative was recommended in 2023 (\cref{sec:metrology}).

\textcite{mihevc2025} operationalise this protocol in a real telemonitoring device, and their description constitutes the most precise specification at our disposal: patients took two readings in the morning and in the evening, after five minutes of rest in a seated position, with back and arm supported. A one-minute interval was programmed by the device between the two readings, and the second was retained as valid.

Two elements of this description deserve to be retained for the design. First, the interval between the two readings is imposed by the device and not left to the patient's initiative, which removes a major source of non-compliance with the protocol. Second, the validity rule---retain the second reading---is applied automatically, and not by a computation demanded of the patient.

For establishing the reference value at the assessment time points, the authors apply a stricter rule still: the second morning reading and the second evening reading are averaged over seven days. This is the direct application of the variance-reduction logic set out in \cref{sec:variability}: with fourteen retained measurements, the standard error of the estimator of the usual pressure is divided by $\sqrt{14} \approx 3.7$ relative to an isolated measurement.

\subsubsection{Capillary blood glucose self-monitoring}

\textcite{mihevc2025} describe a glycaemic protocol articulated in two regimes.

In the \emph{routine} regime, the patient performs two measurements: one in the morning while fasting and one ninety minutes after lunch.

In the \emph{verification} regime, triggered by an abnormal value, the patient performs a one-day glycaemic profile comprising six measurements: fasting, 90 minutes after breakfast, before lunch, 90 minutes after lunch, before dinner, and 90 minutes after dinner.

The therapeutic targets retained, adjusted for an elderly population, are a fasting glucose below 7~mmol/L and a postprandial glucose below 10~mmol/L. For Cameroonian practice, which usually expresses blood glucose in grams per litre, these thresholds correspond to about 1.26~g/L and 1.80~g/L, the conversion factor being $1\ \text{mmol/L} \approx 0.18\ \text{g/L}$.

The blood pressure target retained is a home pressure below 135/85~mmHg---and not 140/90~mmHg---in accordance with the convention whereby self-measurement thresholds are five points below consultation thresholds, the absence of a white-coat effect lowering the values measured at home.

\subsubsection{The decision architecture of the joint protocol}\label{sec:triage}

The most directly exploitable contribution of \textcite{mihevc2025} is their three-level classification system, applied symmetrically to both conditions (\cref{tab:triage-levels}).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Three-level classification of \textcite{mihevc2025}.}\label{tab:triage-levels}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{1.7cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.6cm} X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Code} & \textbf{Interpretation} & \textbf{Action triggered} \\
\midrule
Green & normal values & no change to the follow-up protocol \\
Yellow & abnormal values & intensified surveillance: seven-day blood pressure profile, or one-day six-measurement glycaemic profile \\
Red & critically abnormal values & immediate teleconsultation with the general practitioner, further assessment or treatment modification \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The operational thresholds, as presented in Figure~1 of the article, are reproduced in \cref{tab:thresholds}. We have reorganised them by decision level and by meal timing, because the original presentation leaves the reading of the glycaemic intervals ambiguous.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Operational thresholds of the two protocols \parencite{mihevc2025}, reorganised by decision level. BP in mmHg, glucose in mmol/L.}\label{tab:thresholds}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.4cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Level} & \textbf{Blood pressure (current reading)} & \textbf{Blood glucose (current reading)} \\
\midrule
Red (low) & SBP $<$ 110 or DBP $<$ 60 & $<$ 4 (any timing) \\
Green & 110--134 / 60--84 & 4--7 before a meal; 7--10 after a meal \\
Yellow & 135--174 / 85--104 & 7.1--10 before a meal; 10.1--14 after a meal \\
Red (high) & SBP $\geq$ 175 or DBP $\geq$ 105 & $>$ 10 before a meal; $>$ 14 after a meal \\
\midrule
On the profile mean & $<$ 110/60 or $\geq$ 135/85 $\Rightarrow$ teleconsultation & $>$ 7 before a meal or $>$ 10 after a meal $\Rightarrow$ teleconsultation \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

This architecture possesses three remarkable properties, which it is appropriate to state explicitly because they directly orient the design of the proposed system.

\textbf{It is bidirectional.} Low thresholds trigger as much as high ones. For diabetes this is an obvious clinical necessity---hypoglycaemia is an acute risk---but the device applies the same logic to blood pressure, hypotension in the treated elderly patient being a risk of falls. A system detecting only elevated values would be clinically incomplete.

\textbf{It is two-stage.} An isolated abnormal value does not trigger a clinical action but an additional collection of information. Only in the light of the profile mean is the teleconsultation triggered. This is exactly the operational answer to the variance problem set out in \cref{sec:variability}: the device distinguishes noise from trend by increasing $n$ before deciding, rather than by lowering the threshold (\cref{fig:triage}).

\textbf{It is economical in human attention.} Only the red class mobilises a physician. The yellow class mobilises the patient. In a context where, as \textcite{dodoo2022} establish, professionals perceive telemonitoring as an additional workload (\cref{sec:digital-barriers}), this distribution is a condition of adoption and not a refinement.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=0.55cm and 0.9cm,
  st/.style={draw,rounded corners,align=center,font=\footnotesize,minimum height=0.85cm,minimum width=2.5cm},
  dec/.style={draw,diamond,aspect=2,align=center,font=\footnotesize,inner sep=1pt,fill=cGrey!12},
  ar/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.5mm]},thick}]
  \node[st,fill=cGrey!12] (meas) {Routine reading\\ (low frequency)};
  \node[dec,right=of meas] (cl) {Class?};
  \node[st,fill=green!14,above right=0.5cm and 1.0cm of cl] (gr) {\textbf{Green}\\ no change};
  \node[st,fill=yellow!28,right=1.0cm of cl] (ye) {\textbf{Yellow}\\ verification profile\\ (7 days / 6 readings)};
  \node[st,fill=cAfrica!18,below right=0.5cm and 1.0cm of cl] (re) {\textbf{Red}\\ immediate\\ teleconsultation};
  \node[st,fill=cRef!12,right=1.0cm of ye] (mn) {Profile mean\\ vs target};
  \draw[ar] (meas) -- (cl);
  \draw[ar] (cl) -- (gr);
  \draw[ar] (cl) -- (ye);
  \draw[ar] (cl) -- (re);
  \draw[ar] (ye) -- (mn);
  \draw[ar] (mn.south) |- node[below right,font=\scriptsize,pos=0.75]{off target} (re.east);
  \draw[ar] (mn.north) |- node[above right,font=\scriptsize,pos=0.75]{on target} (gr.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The two-stage triggering architecture of \textcite{mihevc2025}. An isolated abnormal value increases the number of measurements instead of mobilising a clinician; only the mean of the verification profile reaches a clinical decision.}\label{fig:triage}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{The question of frequency}

The sources diverge on the frequency of the routine regime, and this divergence calls for clarification before any reuse.

The abstract and the body of the article of \textcite{mihevc2025} indicate that participants measured their blood pressure twice a week and their blood glucose once a week. Figure~1 of the same article, by contrast, carries the labels ``weekly blood pressure measurements'' and ``monthly blood glucose measurements''. These two statements are incompatible. We retain the textual description, confirmed by the abstract, and we signal the figure as erroneous, without being able to settle the matter definitively without recourse to the separately published protocol.%
\verify{Resolve this frequency divergence against the published protocol of \textcite{mihevc2025} (ISRCTN31471852) before reusing either value as a design parameter.}

The frequency trade-off must in any event be posed explicitly, because it sets two contrary requirements against each other. Variance reduction argues for maximal frequency, since $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\mu) = \sigma_\varepsilon^2 / n$. Adherence argues for minimal frequency, every measurement demanded being an occasion for abandonment. The device of \textcite{mihevc2025} resolves this tension by modulation: a low routine frequency, sufficient for surveillance, and a high frequency triggered only when a signal justifies it. This is a solution the proposed system should adopt, because it optimises both criteria simultaneously instead of sacrificing one of them.

One residual ambiguity of the threshold table must be noted, because any implementation will have to resolve it: the blood pressure rules are stated on two variables, and the source does not say what happens when the two disagree---a systolic value in the yellow band together with a diastolic value in the low red band, for instance. The safe convention, which we adopt, is that the most severe of the two classifications prevails, red before yellow and yellow before green. This is our decision, not a statement of the source.

\subsection{Consequences of the metrological constraint}\label{sec:metrology-consequences}

The WHO position on measuring devices (\cref{sec:metrology}) has three consequences for the design.


Three consequences must be explicitly assumed:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item The ``mobile sensors'' component cannot rely on cuffless devices for blood pressure measurement. These can provide context variables---physical activity, heart rate, sleep---useful as model covariates, but blood pressure must come from a validated automated upper-arm monitor. This restriction does not amputate the project: it moves it from the ambition of unconstrained continuous measurement to that of reliable repeated measurement, which is what the clinical protocol actually requires.
  \item The list of admissible devices must be restricted to devices on a recognised validation register, and this criterion must be part of the system specification.
  \item The cost of the device, higher for validated devices, must be integrated into the economic feasibility analysis alongside the cost of treatment, which the WHO puts at 5 to 10 US dollars per patient per year for quality-assured medicines, amlodipine and hydrochlorothiazide generally being available at 0.01 dollar per tablet or less, but with prices varying more than tenfold between countries and mark-ups recorded in Kenya exceeding 340\% for amlodipine and 280\% for hydrochlorothiazide.
\end{enumerate}
